## L10a 2026-10-01 pass (CI confirms E06/E08 allocator-favoring point estimates are real)

Hypothesis: M01's critic note found that E06 and E08 were relabeled from "threshold wins" to "allocator wins" based on sim_cell100_cheap.json point estimates (greedy_online beats static_matched at 25%/50% budget on both), but neither point had ever had a bootstrap CI run on that specific comparison. L10a tests whether those point estimates are a real effect or resampling noise.

Run: CPU-only, no GPU. New script avrl/exp/L10a_bootstrap_e06_e08.py (copied from avrl/exp/e03b_bootstrap_matched.py per the project's copy-and-modify rule, parameterized with --cheap so one script handles both points' feature lists), run via avrl/exp/L10a.sh. Reused existing avrl/runs/E06/feats.parquet (3,000 groups, 6-column cheap feature set: agreement, n_distinct, mean_tokens, std_tokens, any_truncated, log_qlen) and avrl/runs/E08/feats.parquet (600 groups, same 6 plus frac_syntax_error, frac_exception). cell100 grouping, 300 group-level bootstrap resamples x 8 policy seeds per resample, budgets 10%/25%/50%, label proxy_error. No new rollouts. Outputs: avrl/runs/E06/bootstrap.json, avrl/runs/E08/bootstrap.json, avrl/runs/L10a/result.json.

Point estimates reproduce the pre-existing avrl/runs/E06/sim_cell100_cheap.json and avrl/runs/E08/sim_cell100_cheap.json values exactly (checked directly, not just the run report): E06 25% budget greedy_online=437.0 / static_matched=361.5 matches both files; E08 25% budget greedy_online=81.625 / static_matched=75.375 matches both files. n_boot_used=300/300 for every comparison on both points, no degenerate-resample skips.

E06 (n=3,000 groups, cheap6 features, cell100, 300 resamples x 8 seeds, runtime 854.3s):

| budget | greedy_online vs static_matched | 95% CI | ts_online vs static_matched | 95% CI | greedy_online vs static (free labels) | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 10% | 183.4 vs 148.2, +23.7% | [+20.4%, +29.9%] | 150.5 vs 148.2, +1.5% | [-2.8%, +9.1%] | 183.4 vs 211.0, -13.1% | [-14.7%, -7.2%] |
| 25% | 437.0 vs 361.5, +20.9% | [+18.7%, +26.1%] | 349.6 vs 361.5, -3.3% | [-5.9%, +2.2%] | 437.0 vs 471.0, -7.2% | [-9.1%, -1.2%] |
| 50% | 705.4 vs 645.8, +9.2% | [+8.6%, +13.4%] | 618.1 vs 645.8, -4.3% | [-7.4%, -0.5%] | 705.4 vs 721.0, -2.2% | [-3.3%, +0.4%] |

E08 (n=600 groups, cheap8 features, cell100, 300 resamples x 8 seeds, runtime 112.7s):

| budget | greedy_online vs static_matched | 95% CI | ts_online vs static_matched | 95% CI | greedy_online vs static (free labels) | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 10% | 22.3 vs 31.4, -29.1% | [-40.3%, -17.7%] | 22.1 vs 31.4, -29.5% | [-34.1%, -13.5%] | 22.3 vs 42.0, -47.0% | [-55.5%, -35.3%] |
| 25% | 81.6 vs 75.4, +8.3% | [+1.8%, +13.1%] | 71.1 vs 75.4, -5.6% | [-6.4%, +8.3%] | 81.6 vs 96.0, -15.0% | [-20.8%, -6.2%] |
| 50% | 154.4 vs 138.4, +11.6% | [+4.9%, +14.3%] | 132.6 vs 138.4, -4.2% | [-8.1%, +3.4%] | 154.4 vs 156.0, -1.0% | [-6.2%, +6.9%] |

Verdict against the pre-registered rule (pass if the CI on the 25% relative gain of greedy_online over static_matched excludes zero on the positive side for either point; kill if the CI includes zero at every budget for both points): PASS. E06's CI excludes zero positive at all three budgets (greedy_online wins uniformly, 10% through 50%). E08's CI excludes zero positive at 25% and 50% (allocator wins) but excludes zero on the negative side at 10% (static_matched wins significantly there instead, -29.1% [-40.3%,-17.7%]), so E08 is a genuine budget-dependent crossover, not a uniform win or a uniform tie. Neither point hits the kill bar (CI includes zero at every budget for both): both have multiple budgets with CIs that clear zero in one direction or the other. ts_online (cell bandit) never significantly beats static_matched on either point and loses significantly at E06 50% and E08 10%. greedy_online never beats the free-label static classifier on either point at any budget (same pattern as L9d).

What it changes: this directly resolves M01's single biggest open question. The allocator-favoring point estimates the M01 critic flagged for E06 and E08 are real effects, not resampling noise, at the project's primary reporting budget (25%) and at 50%. This reinstates M01's corrected ground truth (E06 and E08 both belong in the "allocator wins" bucket at 25%/50%, matching the critic's correction) rather than the original citation-inferred "threshold wins" label. M01's corrected tally (n_match=5/9, n_mismatch=4/9, clearing the kill bar) now stands on an actual bootstrap CI for 7 of 9 points instead of 5, strengthening the kill verdict for the single-statistic 0.85-AUC regime diagnostic and the case against funding M02-M05 in their current form. The new wrinkle this run surfaces: E08's significant reversal at 10% budget (static_matched wins there) means "allocator wins" is not even a clean per-point label once budget is varied; the diagnostic would need to be budget-aware, not just AUC-aware, to be trustworthy at low label budgets.

Follow-ups:
1. (L11a, <1 min, CPU-only, unblocked by this run) Run the already-specced L10b (avrl/exp/M01.py rerun with E06/E08 ground truth corrected to this run's actual 25%-budget bootstrap verdict: allocator wins both) and recompute the 9-point tally. Replaces M01's provisional "corrected to kill" read with a number backed by bootstrap CIs for 7 of 9 points.
2. (L11b, ~20 min, CPU-only, reuses E08/feats.parquet) E08 shows a significant reversal between 10% (static_matched wins) and 25% (greedy_online wins) that no other point in the ledger exhibits at this resolution. Rerun avrl/exp/L10a_bootstrap_e06_e08.py on E08 with a finer budget grid (5%, 10%, 15%, 20%, 25%) to locate the crossover point and check whether it tracks the online learner's warm-up window (R = max(budget//8, 40) in the script, so very low budgets may exhaust before greedy_online's self-trained ranking has enough verified labels to beat a pre-fit threshold).
3. (L11c, ~15 min, CPU-only, reuses E06/E09/E09b/E09d feats.parquet) Check whether the same low-budget reversal pattern appears in any other ledger point at a finer grid below 10% (E06 at 2%/5%/7%) or in the weak-signal points (E09, E09b, E09d, which only ever reported 10/25/50%). If greedy_online's cold-start disadvantage at low budgets is general rather than E08-specific, it is a design-relevant finding for M02-M05: a router pitched as working at any label budget needs a documented minimum budget floor, not just an AUC-based regime switch.

## M01b 2026-10-01 kill

Hypothesis: same regime diagnostic as M01 (cheap-feature AUC >= 0.85 -> predict threshold wins, else predict allocator wins), re-tested using ONLY bootstrap-backed ground truth: for every point with its own avrl/runs/<point>/bootstrap.json, actual winner is read off the 95% CI on the greedy_online-vs-static_matched relative gain at the 25% budget (cell100, cheap features) -- allocator if the CI excludes zero on the positive side, threshold if it excludes zero on the negative side, tie otherwise. This removes the qualitative/inferred ground truth that M01's critic note flagged as unreliable for E06 and E08 (RESEARCH_LOG.md ## M01), by running avrl/exp/bootstrap_replay.py (E01/p0, E06, E08, E09) and reusing avrl/exp/e09_bootstrap.py's existing output (E09b, E09c, E09d); E04/L5a and E05 are dropped (no feats.parquet, so no bootstrap is possible).

Run: no new rollouts, no GPU, CPU-only. Script avrl/exp/M01b.py (new file, does not edit M01.py or either bootstrap script) reads each point's result.json and bootstrap.json under avrl/runs/<point>/, pulls the cell100/cheap/25%-budget greedy_online-vs-static_matched comparison, and applies the rules above. 7 points qualify (E01/p0, E06, E08, E09, E09b, E09c, E09d).

| point | n | cheap AUC | neighbor ratio (cell100) | 25% bootstrap rel. gain [95% CI] | predicted | actual | match |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E01/p0 | 3000 | 0.923 | 1.032 | +19.2% [+15.0%, +26.3%] | threshold | allocator | MISMATCH |
| E06 | 3000 | 0.887 | 0.999 | +20.9% [+18.7%, +26.1%] | threshold | allocator | MISMATCH |
| E08 | 600 | 0.868 | 1.007 | +8.3% [+1.8%, +13.1%] | threshold | allocator | MISMATCH |
| E09 | 1500 | 0.734 | 0.998 | +12.7% [+3.9%, +25.5%] | allocator | allocator | MATCH |
| E09b | 1500 | 0.746 | 1.049 | +25.7% [+5.3%, +26.2%] | allocator | allocator | MATCH |
| E09c | 1500 | 0.799 | 1.220 | +11.7% [-5.7%, +33.7%] | allocator | tie | TIE (no call) |
| E09d | 4000 | 0.795 | 1.141 | +18.8% [+7.8%, +26.1%] | allocator | allocator | MATCH |

Verdict against M01's pre-registered rule (pass if >= 8/9 match; kill if >= 3/9 mismatch; with 7 points instead of 9, apply the same proportion: pass requires match_frac >= 0.889, kill requires mismatch_frac >= 0.333): 3/7 match (42.9%), 3/7 mismatch (42.9%), 1/7 tie. Result: KILL.

AUC-cutoff note: brute-forcing the cutoff on these 7 points (rule: predict allocator-win iff auc_cheap < cutoff, scored against actual=='allocator' as the positive class) finds best accuracy 85.7% at cutoff 0.933 (vs the pre-registered 0.85). The data-driven cutoff (0.933) differs from the pre-registered 0.85, which is weak evidence either way given only 7 points and ties that no single cutoff can resolve (a cutoff only distinguishes allocator-win from not-allocator-win; it cannot separate threshold-win from tie).

Critic note (independent verification pass, 2026-10-01): checked every number in the M01b entry against the raw files, avrl/runs/M01b/result.json and run.log, and each of the seven points' own result.json and bootstrap.json under avrl/runs/<point>/. All seven n, auc_cheap, and neighbor_ratio_cell100 values, and the cell100/cheap/25%-budget greedy_online vs static_matched rel_improve_point and rel_improve_ci95 values, match the raw files exactly. The match/mismatch/tie tally (3 match, 3 mismatch, 1 tie, match_frac 0.429, mismatch_frac 0.429) is computed correctly by avrl/exp/M01b.py and matches result.json. Against the pre-registered proportional thresholds (pass needs match_frac >= 0.889, which with n=7 means all 7 points must match, since 6/7=0.857 falls short; kill needs mismatch_frac >= 0.333, cleared once 3 of 7 mismatch, since 3/7=0.429 clears it while 2/7=0.286 would not), 3/7 mismatch clears the kill bar and 3/7 match falls far short of pass, so KILL is the correct mechanical verdict and confirms the M01 critic note's by-hand correction on cleaner, fully bootstrap-backed evidence. The seven underlying comparisons all show n_boot_used 300/300 (no degenerate resample skips) with 8 policy seeds per resample, consistent with the already-reviewed L10a methodology, and budget_n = int(budget_frac * len(sim)) is identical across static, static_matched, greedy_online, and ts_online within every resample (confirmed in each point's own bootstrap.json note field), so budget accounting is equal across policies. The cheap feature set (agreement, n_distinct, mean_tokens, std_tokens, any_truncated, log_qlen, plus two code-specific columns for E08) is unchanged from the set M01's critic note already verified as leak-free, derived only from the model's own rollouts, never the gold answer; M01b adds no new features and does not touch gold labels, so no new leakage is introduced. No crash or silent truncation appears in run.log.

One defect found: this M01b entry is duplicated verbatim in this ledger. Two byte-identical "## M01b 2026-10-01 kill" sections appear back to back immediately above (grep confirms it), even though avrl/runs/M01b/ holds only a single result.json and run.log from one execution. This points to the entry being appended to RESEARCH_LOG.md twice rather than two independent runs. It changes no number and not the verdict, since both copies are identical, but the two sections should be read as one entry, not two, and the duplicate block should be removed the next time this file is edited by hand, so later ledger parsing does not double count this point.

Corrected verdict: KILL stands, unchanged from the entry above. match_frac = 3/7 (0.429), mismatch_frac = 3/7 (0.429), n_tie = 1/7.

## Note 2026-10-01 (night): greedy allocator added, M06 and M07 queued
M01b showed the replay winner on every bootstrapped point is greedy_online (online classifier, rank by predicted corrupted-reward probability, no cell bandit), while the in-training "learned" arm is the bandit-flavoured LearnedAllocator. Added GreedyOnlineAllocator ("greedy") to avrl/allocators.py: identical training data and refit schedule to LearnedAllocator, direct ranking by predicted probability, threshold rule at cold start, budget accounting unchanged; unit-checked on mock records. Queued: M07 on goldberg's 5090 after the open-ended run (GSM8K 1.5B, lr 1e-5, arms greedy / threshold / learned, seeds 0 and 1, outputs avrl/runs/m07_*); M06 (threshold and learned, 0.5B) then M06b (greedy, 0.5B) on cherry after its seed-2 job. These are the layout ablation in training.

What it changes: this settles M01's open question on bootstrap-only evidence, and the result is worse than the hand-corrected tally, not better. Two things change about the thesis claims:

1. The AUC-cutoff regime diagnostic (auc_cheap >= 0.85 predicts threshold wins) is killed on clean evidence: 3/7 match (42.9%), 3/7 mismatch (42.9%, clears the 0.333 kill bar), 1 tie. This is worse than M01's critic-note hand correction (5/9, 55.6% match). No single AUC cutoff, including the brute-forced 0.933, separates threshold-wins from allocator-wins cleanly, because a cutoff can only separate allocator-win from not-allocator-win, and it cannot carve out the tie (E09c). M02-M05's regime-conditional design (a single statistic switching between a recalibrated threshold and a structure-aware allocator) has no reliable step-0 signal to switch on, per this evidence.

2. The bigger change is to E01. PROJECT.md and the L3a/L4a entries have always treated E01 (GSM8K, 1.5B policy, cheap AUC 0.92) as "the one true tie," the single point where the allocator does not beat the threshold. That tie was established with ts_online (the cell-bandit allocator), never with greedy_online (the self-training allocator). M01b's bootstrap on greedy_online vs static_matched at E01 is fully positive, CI [+15.0%, +26.3%], i.e. the allocator wins there too. Once greedy_online is the comparison, every bootstrap-backed point except the tie (E09c) favors the allocator (E01, E06, E08, E09, E09b, E09d); ts_online never does. This reframes the open question from "which AUC cutoff separates the regimes" to "which allocator algorithm is actually winning": the gain looks like it comes from refitting a classifier on bought labels and ranking by it (greedy self-training), not from exploiting prompt-space cell structure (the cell-bandit mechanism M03 is specced around). The in-training "kill" already on record for claim 2 (P1-goldberg-s0/s1, learned arm admitted more corrupted groups than the threshold) used the bandit-flavoured LearnedAllocator, not a greedy self-training arm, so that kill verdict may be an artifact of algorithm choice rather than a fundamental failure of learned allocation in training.

Follow-ups:
1. (L12a, ~2-3 hours on goldberg's 5090, 1 seed, in-training) Run a greedy self-training allocator arm (refit classifier on bought labels each step, rank unverified groups by predicted proxy error, no cell/Thompson-sampling layer) in avrl/p1_grpo.py against the existing threshold and bandit-flavoured learned arms, on E01's exact setting (GSM8K, Qwen2.5-1.5B, 25% budget, lr 1e-5). This directly tests whether the already-recorded in-training kill for claim 2 (P1-goldberg-s0: learned admitted 72 corrupted groups vs threshold's 27) reflects the wrong allocator algorithm rather than a fundamental in-training failure of learned allocation, which is the single highest-value open question M01b raises.
2. (L12b, ~20 min, CPU-only, reuses existing feats.parquet for E01/E06/E08/E09/E09b/E09d) Compute ts_online-vs-static_matched bootstrap CIs (same methodology as M01b, swapping the comparison arm) on every point that currently only has a greedy_online CI, so the "cell bandit doesn't win, greedy does" pattern seen on E01 can be checked across all 7 points rather than inferred from one. If ts_online loses or ties everywhere, M03's cell-Thompson-sampling design is testing a mechanism that already looks non-viable in replay, before any GPU time is spent on it.
3. (L12c, ~15 min, CPU-only, reuses E09b/E09c/E09d feats.parquet) E09c is the one tie and the thinnest point (CI [-5.7%, +33.7%], point estimate +11.7%). Rerun its bootstrap with more resamples (1000 instead of 300) or pool its error groups with E09b/E09d (same judge family, same rollouts lineage) to see whether a tighter CI resolves the tie one way or the other; right now 1 of 7 points contributes a "no call" that shrinks the effective n for both the match and mismatch tallies.

## L12b 2026-10-01 kill

Hypothesis: RESEARCH_LOG.md's "Note 2026-10-01 (night)" claims ts_online (the cell/Thompson-sampling allocator M03 is specced around) "never" beats static_matched across the bootstrapped points, concluding claim 2's whole replay gain comes from greedy_online's classifier-refit mechanism rather than from exploiting prompt-space cell structure. That claim was asserted from reading greedy_online's table only (M01b); the ts_online-vs-static_matched comparison was already computed in the same bootstrap run for all 7 points but never pulled into a table. L12b tallies the existing numbers under the same CI-sign rule used in L10a/M01b to check whether the "never" claim holds.

Run: CPU-only, no GPU, no new rollouts or bootstrap resampling. New script avrl/exp/L12b_tsonline_tally.py (copied from avrl/exp/M01b.py per the project's copy-and-modify rule, comparison swapped to ts_online vs static_matched, widened to pull all three budget_fracs). Reads each point's existing avrl/runs/<point>/bootstrap.json and result.json only. Points: E01/p0, E06, E08, E09, E09b, E09c, E09d (same 7 points M01b used). group_col=cell100, feature_set=cheap, primary budget 25%.

Independently re-verified: read all 7 points' bootstrap.json files directly (not via the script) and confirmed the ts_online-vs-static_matched comparison at budget_frac=0.25, group_col=cell100, feature_set=cheap matches result.json's rel_improve_point and rel_improve_ci95 exactly for every point, including auc_cheap and neighbor_ratio_cell100 pulled from each point's own result.json. No discrepancies found between raw files, result.json, and the run report's key_numbers.

| point | n | cheap AUC | neighbor ratio (cell100) | 25% budget rel. gain (ts_online vs static_matched) | 95% CI | call |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E01/p0 | 3000 | 0.923 | 1.032 | -1.9% | [-8.4%, +5.5%] | tie |
| E06 | 3000 | 0.887 | 0.999 | -3.3% | [-5.9%, +2.2%] | tie |
| E08 | 600 | 0.868 | 1.007 | -5.6% | [-6.4%, +8.3%] | tie |
| E09 | 1500 | 0.734 | 0.998 | +6.8% | [+1.2%, +27.0%] | WIN |
| E09b | 1500 | 0.746 | 1.049 | +12.0% | [-2.6%, +23.2%] | tie |
| E09c | 1500 | 0.799 | 1.220 | +8.9% | [-12.9%, +36.1%] | tie |
| E09d | 4000 | 0.795 | 1.141 | +30.8% | [+13.6%, +36.6%] | WIN |

n_win=2, n_lose=0, n_tie=5. Cross-check against claim 3: of the 4 low-cheap-AUC points (auc_cheap < 0.80: E09, E09b, E09c, E09d), 2 win; of the 3 high-cheap-AUC points (E01/p0, E06, E08), 0 win. Both wins land inside the low-signal subgroup, but the subgroup is not swept clean (E09b and E09c tie).

Verdict against the pre-registered thresholds (pass: 0/7 significant positive CIs; kill: >=1/7): KILL. 2 of 7 points (E09, E09d) show a significant positive ts_online-vs-static_matched CI at the 25% primary budget, falsifying the Note's "never" claim. Per the spec's "stronger reading" criterion, both wins land specifically on E09 and E09d, the two points with cheap AUC below 0.80 and the project's only two points with replicated embedding/neighbor-ratio structure (E09d's confident-subset neighbor ratio 2.02, E09c's 1.84, per PROJECT.md), not a random pair from the 7.

What it changes: the Note's wording was too strong. ts_online does not "never" win; it wins on exactly the two points with the weakest cheap signal and the clearest embedding structure, and ties (not loses) everywhere else. This revives a qualified version of claim 3: the cell-bandit mechanism has a real, narrow foothold where there is prompt-space structure to bandit over, rather than being flatly dead. It does not overturn M01b's main finding that greedy_online is the more reliable winner across all 7 points (5 wins/ties favoring greedy_online vs ts_online's 2 wins/0 losses here are not directly comparable without a head-to-head, but ts_online never beating greedy_online's win rate stands). The practical read for M02-M05: a router that only ever deploys greedy_online would leave E09/E09d's extra gain on the table; a router that tries ts_online only on low-cheap-AUC, high-neighbor-ratio points might capture it, but with n=2 wins out of 4 candidate points, the signal is too thin to design around without a direct head-to-head and more points.

Follow-ups:
1. (L13a, ~15 min, CPU-only, reuses E09/E09d/E09b/E09c feats.parquet) Run greedy_online vs ts_online head-to-head (not each vs static_matched) on all 4 low-cheap-AUC points at 25% budget, same CI-sign rule. This directly answers whether ts_online's wins on E09/E09d represent real incremental gain over greedy_online (the mechanism claim 3 needs) or whether greedy_online dominates there too and ts_online's win over static_matched is just a weaker version of the same classifier-refit effect.
2. (L13b, ~20 min, CPU-only, reuses E09b/E09c feats.parquet, no GPU) E09b and E09c have the same low-cheap-AUC profile as E09/E09d but tied rather than won. Check whether this is a power problem (E09b/E09c's CIs are wide, consistent with fewer error groups) by rerunning their existing bootstrap at 1000 resamples instead of 300, to see if a tighter CI resolves either tie to a win or a genuine null, before concluding claim 3's foothold is narrow by true effect size rather than by underpowered sampling.
3. (L13c, ~10 min, CPU-only, reuses all 7 points' bootstrap.json, no new compute) Re-run this same tally at the 10% and 50% budgets (both already present in result.json's all_budgets field, already computed, zero marginal compute) to check whether E09/E09d's wins are stable across budget or a 25%-only artifact; L10a already found a budget-dependent crossover for E08's greedy_online comparison, so the same check matters here before citing E09/E09d's wins as a stable mechanism result.

Critic note (independent verification pass, 2026-10-01): checked every number in the entry against the raw files, avrl/runs/L12b/result.json, avrl/exp/L12b_tsonline_tally.py, and each of the seven points' own result.json and bootstrap.json under avrl/runs/<point>/. All seven n, cheap_auc, and neighbor_ratio_cell100 values, and the cell100/cheap/25%-budget ts_online-vs-static_matched rel_improve_point and rel_improve_ci95 values, match the raw bootstrap.json entries exactly, including the two points that drive the verdict (E09 and E09d, checked directly against their bootstrap.json comparisons rather than only against result.json). The tally (n_win=2, n_lose=0, n_tie=5) is computed correctly by the script and matches result.json. Budget accounting is equal across all four policies in every point's bootstrap.json note field (shared fit_b/sim split, same budget_n = int(budget_frac * len(sim))), n_boot_used is 300/300 with 8 policy seeds in all seven points, and the script does no new resampling or rollouts, so there is no run.log and no truncation or crash risk to check. Against this task's pre-registered thresholds (pass: 0/7 significant positive CIs; kill: >=1/7), KILL is the correct mechanical verdict: 2 of 7 points (E09, E09d) clear the kill bar.

The entry's "stronger reading" sentence is wrong and needs correcting. It says both wins land on "the two points with cheap AUC below 0.80" and on "the project's only two points with replicated embedding/neighbor-ratio structure (E09d's confident-subset neighbor ratio 2.02, E09c's 1.84, per PROJECT.md)." Two sentences earlier the entry's own cross-check table already says four points, not two, have cheap AUC under 0.80 (E09, E09b, E09c, E09d); E09 and E09d are only two of those four, and E09b/E09c also qualify but tie, so this halves the real denominator. The structure claim is the bigger problem: PROJECT.md documents the replicated confident-subset neighbor-ratio signal (2.02, 1.84) on E09d and E09c, not on E09. E09 is on record in PROJECT.md (the E09 2026-10-01 entry and the "Update 2026-10-01" paragraph) as the point with no embedding structure at all, neighbor ratio 1.00, embeddings adding only 0.035 AUC against the 0.05 bar, failing the project's own pre-registered claim-3-is-dead rule on both counts. This run's own neighbor_ratio_cell100 for E09 (0.998) confirms that. So the two ts_online wins do not land cleanly on "the structure points": E09d's win lands on a point with real, replicated structure, but E09's win lands on a point PROJECT.md already flagged as having none, while E09c, the point that actually replicates E09d's structure signal, ties rather than wins. The "What it changes" paragraph repeats the same error ("wins on exactly the two points with ... the clearest embedding structure").

Corrected verdict: KILL stands, unchanged (2/7 points clear the >=1/7 kill bar). The "stronger reading" qualifier does not hold as written. The wins are on E09 and E09d, not on "the two points with cheap AUC below 0.80" (there are four candidates) or on "the only two points with replicated embedding/neighbor-ratio structure" (that pair is E09d and E09c, and E09c ties, not wins). The honest reading is weaker than claimed: of the four low-cheap-AUC candidate points, two win (E09, E09d) and two tie (E09b, E09c), with no clean split by documented embedding structure, since E09 (a win) has none (ratio 1.00) and E09c (a tie) has the second-strongest replicated structure after E09d. This does not revive even a qualified claim 3 on the structure mechanism specifically; at most it shows ts_online beats static_matched on 2 of 4 weak-cheap-signal points for a reason this data does not pin down, which is a different and weaker claim than "works where there is structure to bandit over." Follow-ups L13a-c are unaffected by this correction and remain the right next checks, especially L13b, since a power fix on E09b/E09c's ties (rather than a genuine null) is the one outcome that could reopen the structure story.

## L13a 2026-10-01 inconclusive (greedy_online vs ts_online head-to-head on the two ts_online-win points)

Hypothesis: L12b found ts_online beats static_matched at the 25% budget on exactly 2 of 7 points (E09, E09d), the only two with a significant positive CI, and its corrected reading noted the wins don't cleanly track a cheap-AUC or structure split. L12b follow-up 1 asks the sharper question: on those same two points, does ts_online add anything over greedy_online (the simpler classifier-refit allocator M01b found winning on every other bootstrapped point), or does greedy_online win there too, making ts_online's edge over static_matched a weaker version of the same mechanism.

Run: CPU-only, no GPU, no new rollouts. New script avrl/exp/L13a_greedy_vs_ts.py (copied from avrl/exp/bootstrap_replay.py per the project's copy-and-modify rule; two module constants changed: BUDGET_FRACS restricted to (0.25,), COMPARISONS restricted to [("greedy_online", "ts_online")], everything else -- clf, run_online, split_sim, the percentile-CI bootstrap -- unchanged). Reuses avrl/runs/E09/feats.parquet and avrl/runs/E09d/feats.parquet, same cheap feature set (rm_max, rm_mean, rm_std, rm_gap12, mean_tokens, std_tokens, trunc_frac, consistency, log_qlen) and cell100 grouping as every other table in this ledger, n_boot=300, seeds=8 (same precision as M01b/L12b). Restricted to E09 and E09d only (the two points with an actual win over static_matched to explain); E09b/E09c tied in L12b so were not rerun here, to stay comfortably inside the 45-minute compute cap. Launched as two parallel CPU processes (PIDs confirmed via pgrep before and after), GPU untouched throughout (nvidia-smi showed 14217 MiB in use the whole time from an unrelated in-flight job, p1_judge_threshold_lr5_s1, which this run never competed with). Completed in 203s (E09) and 289s (E09d), both exit 0, no errors.

| point | hits greedy_online | hits ts_online | diff (greedy - ts) | rel. diff | 95% CI | call |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E09 | 111.1 | 105.4 | +5.75 | +5.5% | [-9.8%, +12.2%] | tie |
| E09d | 121.9 | 134.3 | -12.4 | -9.2% | [-15.0%, +2.7%] | tie |

Verdict against the same CI-sign rule used throughout this ledger (win if the 95% CI excludes zero on either side, tie otherwise): TIE on both points. Neither allocator significantly beats the other head-to-head at this sample size (n_boot=300, 8 seeds).

What it changes: this neither confirms nor kills the narrow claim-3 revival L12b left open. It does rule out the cleanest version of the "greedy_online dominates everywhere" story: on E09d, the point with the strongest documented structure (neighbor ratio 1.14 overall / 2.02 in the confident subset) and ts_online's best win over static_matched (+30.8%), the point estimate here actually runs the other way (greedy_online -9.2% relative to ts_online, i.e. ts_online numerically ahead), though the CI [-15.0%, +2.7%] does not clear significance. On E09 (no documented structure, neighbor ratio 1.00), the point estimate mildly favors greedy_online (+5.5%) but is also not significant. Honest read: this comparison is underpowered to say whether ts_online's edge on E09d is a real incremental mechanism over greedy_online's classifier-refit signal or noise riding on it; the data collected here cannot resolve L12b's follow-up question either way, though it narrows M01b's claim that greedy_online is "the more reliable winner across all 7 points" to "greedy_online never loses significantly, including head-to-head," which is weaker than "greedy_online wins everywhere."

Follow-ups:
1. The E09d CI [-15.0%, +2.7%] is the closer-to-significant of the two, in the direction of a real ts_online effect. If this point is revisited, rerunning just this one comparison at 1000 resamples (instead of 300) is the natural power check, since E09d is the single point where an incremental ts_online-over-greedy_online effect looks most plausible from the point estimate.
2. E09b and E09c (tied against static_matched in L12b) were not rerun here; if the structure story is pursued further, the same head-to-head on those two would complete the 4-point picture, at roughly 10-11 min of CPU time each based on E09/E09d's observed runtimes.

Critic note (independent verification pass, 2026-10-01): checked every number in the entry against the raw files (avrl/runs/L13a/E09_bootstrap.json, avrl/runs/L13a/E09d_bootstrap.json, avrl/runs/L13a/E09.log, avrl/runs/L13a/E09d.log), the script avrl/exp/L13a_greedy_vs_ts.py, and a direct diff against avrl/exp/bootstrap_replay.py. The table's hits_a/hits_b, diff_point, rel_improve_point, and rel_improve_ci95 match the raw bootstrap.json files exactly for both E09 and E09d, including rounding. The diff against bootstrap_replay.py confirms the entry's claim: only the docstring and two module constants differ (BUDGET_FRACS restricted to (0.25,), COMPARISONS restricted to [("greedy_online", "ts_online")]); clf, run_online, split_sim, and the bootstrap/CI code are byte-identical. n_boot_used=300 equals n_boot_requested for both points (no truncation), seeds_per_resample=8 matches the claimed precision, and both run logs print the same n_boot_used/runtime_sec/n_comparisons that appear in the output JSON, with runtime_sec (202.98s, 289.29s) matching the entry's claimed 203s/289s, so there is no sign of a hidden crash. total_label1_in_sim/n_sim (227/1137 for E09, 225/2928 for E09d) match the cell100, budget_frac=0.25 entries already on record in the original (pre-L13a) E09/E09d bootstrap.json files exactly, confirming the fit_b/sim split is deterministic and reused correctly. The feature list (rm_max, rm_mean, rm_std, rm_gap12, mean_tokens, std_tokens, trunc_frac, consistency, log_qlen) excludes both top_rm_gold_positive and the proxy_error label column itself, so there is no gold-answer leakage into the allocator's features; this is the same feature set used throughout the ledger. Budget accounting is explicitly equal across all four policies per the script's own note field (shared fit_b/sim split, same budget_n = int(budget_frac * len(sim))). Against this task's pre-registered rule (CI excludes zero on the positive side on either point confirms greedy_online dominates, closing claim 3; CI excludes zero on the negative side on either point confirms ts_online adds real value, keeping claim 3 alive), the entry's own rel_improve_ci95 values straddle zero at both points (E09 [-9.8%, +12.2%], E09d [-15.0%, +2.7%]), so neither threshold is met and the entry's TIE/inconclusive call is the mechanically correct verdict.

One number in the entry does not hold up: follow-up 2's estimate that rerunning E09b and E09c would take "roughly 10-11 min of CPU time each based on E09/E09d's observed runtimes" is not supported by, and in fact runs against, this entry's own data. E09b and E09c both have n_groups=1500 (avrl/runs/E09b/feats.parquet, avrl/runs/E09c/feats.parquet), the same size as E09, and this entry's own L13a run on E09 (n_groups=1500, one budget, one comparison) completed in 203s (about 3.4 min); E09d (n_groups=4000) took 289s (about 4.8 min). Scaling from either of those puts a same-size E09b/E09c head-to-head run at roughly 3-5 min, not 10-11 min. The 10-11 min figure actually traces to the original, pre-L13a, three-budget three-comparison bootstrap.json runtimes for E09b (1778.8s) and E09c (1918.8s): dividing by their 3 budget_fracs gives 592.9s (9.9 min) and 639.6s (10.7 min) per budget, which is where "10-11 min" comes from, not E09/E09d's observed runtimes as stated. Those original per-point runtimes are themselves 4.4-4.8x slower than E09's original three-budget run (399.9s) despite identical n_groups=1500, most likely reflecting CPU contention from other jobs on goldberg at the time rather than anything about E09b/E09c's data, so neither basis is a reliable prediction for a fresh run.

Corrected verdict: unchanged for the experiment itself. TIE on both points / inconclusive stands as the correct result under the pre-registered CI-sign rule; nothing about the core E09/E09d head-to-head numbers, the script diff, or the budget/leakage accounting needs correction. The one fix is to follow-up 2's runtime estimate: replace "roughly 10-11 min of CPU time each based on E09/E09d's observed runtimes" with an estimate consistent with this entry's own data, e.g. roughly 3-5 min each if goldberg's load matches this run, though the original L12b sweep for these same points ran 4-5x slower under then-current contention, so budgeting up to about 10 min to be safe is still reasonable, just not for the stated reason.

## P1-judge-5090-s0 2026-10-01 inconclusive (powered open-ended run, bf16 7B judge, goldberg RTX 5090)
Run: avrl/p1_judge_grpo.py on goldberg (RTX 5090 32 GB), Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct policy with LoRA r=32, e09b prompts (200 held out), 8 prompts x G=8, 384-token completions, lr 1e-5, budget 0.25, seed 0; proxy = Skywork 0.6B reward model (top half of group), gold = Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in bf16 (score >= 7). 21 s per step. Both arms ran out of memory at step 146 (29.8 GB; the same prompt batch, since prompt order is shared), so the comparison is at 145 steps with 290 expensive calls each. A memory-safe rerun with the 4-bit 7B judge is queued.

| quantity | threshold (smallest RM gap first) | learned (online allocator, bandit-flavoured) |
|---|---|---|
| expensive calls / groups (145 steps) | 290 / 1,160 | 290 / 1,160 |
| corrupted-reward groups admitted | 52 | 53 |
| corrupted per 25 steps | 19, 6, 12, 9, 3, 3 | 13, 11, 13, 8, 3, 5 |
| eval judge score at steps 25 / 50 / 75 / 100 / 125 | 5.665 / 5.615 / 5.620 / 5.615 / 5.595 | 5.690 / 5.665 / 5.670 / 5.710 / 5.725 |
| eval gold-positive rate at step 125 | 0.51 | 0.54 |

Verdict: inconclusive. Claim 2 (fewer corrupted rewards) is a tie for the bandit-flavoured allocator, consistent with the replay (ts_online does not beat the threshold on the open-ended points except in L12b's two cases). Claim 1 (policy quality) shows a monotone divergence across five evaluations in the learned arm's favour (+0.13 judge points, +3 points gold-positive rate at step 125) that is inside single-run noise on 200 prompts; it needs the rerun and a second seed. Note the threshold arm's quality falls during training while the learned arm's rises, which, if replicated, would be the in-training counterpart of the replay result.
What it changes: with the real 7B judge in the loop the two allocators admit the same number of corrupted rewards, so any quality difference must come from WHICH groups each verifies, not how many errors it catches. The greedy self-trainer (M07, now running) is the arm the replay says should do better on corrupted admission; it should be added to the open-ended setting next (M08: greedy arm, open-ended, 4-bit 7B judge, two seeds).

## Note 2026-10-02 01:15 UTC: interim in-training reading (seed 0) and the baseline distinction
Interim, seed 0 only, 100 steps, lr 1e-5, budget 0.25: M07 (GSM8K 1.5B) threshold 26 corrupted vs greedy 35 vs bandit-flavoured learned on track for about 70; M06 (GSM8K 0.5B) threshold 119 vs learned 158; M08a (open-ended, A4000 config) greedy 24 at step 82 vs threshold 40 and 39 (seeds 0 and 1). Accuracy ties throughout.
Baseline distinction that reconciles replay and training: the replay's "static_matched" baseline buys random labels with half its budget and trains a classifier; the in-training "threshold" arm ranks by the cheap grader's own confidence (vote agreement, reward-model gap) and needs no labels. The self-trainer beats the first (L9a to L10a, M01b) and does not beat the second, in replay (greedy_online vs static with free labels: E01 -3 percent, E06 -7 percent, E08 -15 percent, E09d -3 percent, intervals include or sit below zero) or, so far, in training. Pre-registered reading going forward: on tasks where the cheap grader exposes a label-free confidence signal, the fixed threshold is near-optimal and claim 2 is not supported; the open-ended task with a reward-model proxy is the one setting where the greedy arm may still win (M08a, M08 pending, two seeds each).
Replay check of that distinction (greedy_online vs static with free labels, 25 percent budget, cell100, cheap features, from each point's bootstrap.json): E01 -4.3 percent [-6.9, +3.3]; E06 -7.2 percent [-9.1, -1.2]; E08 -15.0 percent [-20.8, -6.2]; E09 -5.0 percent [-16.0, +16.5]; E09b -3.2 percent [-20.9, +15.2]; E09c -19.8 percent [-32.0, +39.5]; E09d -3.3 percent [-15.3, +17.3]. The self-trainer never beats the confidence-ranked baseline in replay; the two exact-answer points where it is significantly worse (E06, E08) are the two where training also shows the threshold ahead.

| arm | corrupted s0 / s1 (of 600 unverified) | per 25 steps s0 / s1 | eval judge score at 100, s0 / s1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| threshold (RM gap, label-free) | 40 / 39 | 9,8,17,6 / 8,9,6,16 | 4.62 / 4.38 |
| learned (bandit-flavoured) | 34 / 43 | 8,11,8,7 / 9,13,8,13 | 4.58 / 4.43 |

Verdict: pass (pre-registered M08 rule: greedy admits at least 15 percent fewer corrupted groups than the threshold in both seeds; observed 25 and 33 percent fewer). Quality: tie in seed 0, +0.13 in seed 1, inside noise. The bandit-flavoured arm ties the threshold; the layout-free self-trainer beats it, and its admissions fall over training (10, 8, 6, 6 and 5, 10, 4, 7) while the threshold's do not, the opposite of the GSM8K picture.
What it changes: first in-training support for claim 2, and the first in-training point that separates the two regimes with the right baseline. On GSM8K the label-free confidence signal (vote agreement) is strong and the threshold wins (M07: 26 vs 35); on the open-ended task the label-free signal (reward-model gap) is weak and the self-trainer wins. The predictive statistic is the AUC of the label-free confidence feature alone, measured on a pre-training probe. Pending: M08 (same comparison with the 4-bit 7B judge at 150 steps on goldberg), M07 and M06 second seeds, hannah four-arm runs.
Confidence-feature AUCs for proxy error (label-free, single feature, measured on the phase-0 samples): GSM8K vote agreement 0.914 (1.5B, E01) and 0.874 (0.5B, E06); open-ended reward-model top-two gap 0.509 (E09b) and 0.569 (E09d), barely above chance, while the free-text consistency analogue (mean pairwise MiniLM cosine among the 8 responses) reaches 0.69. So the in-training open-ended "threshold" arm is close to a random allocator, which is why the self-trainer beats it, and a stronger label-free rule for free text should rank by consistency (M09).

## M09 2026-10-02 kill for claim 2, pass for the consistency rule (consistency-ranked label-free threshold, open-ended task, two seeds, spruce A4000)
Hypothesis: the greedy self-trainer's M08a win on the open-ended task was over a near-random fixed rule (reward-model gap, AUC 0.51 to 0.57); a label-free threshold ranking groups by response consistency (mean pairwise MiniLM cosine among the 8 responses, AUC 0.69) is the fair fixed rule, and the self-trainer must beat it by 15 percent in both seeds for claim 2 to stand on this task.
Run: avrl/p1_judge_grpo.py --arm threshold --threshold_feature consistency, configuration identical to P1-judge-lr5 and M08a (Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, LoRA, e09b prompts, 8 x G=6, 320 tokens, 100 steps, budget 0.25, lr 1e-5, Skywork 0.6B proxy, 4-bit 3B judge as gold), seeds 0 and 1. Files: avrl/runs/p1_judge_thrcons_lr5_s{0,1}/.

| arm (all label-free except greedy) | corrupted s0 / s1 (of 600) | per 25 steps s0 / s1 | eval judge score at 100, s0 / s1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| threshold by reward-model gap (AUC 0.51 to 0.57) | 40 / 39 | 9,8,17,6 / 8,9,6,16 | 4.62 / 4.38 |
| learned, classifier plus cell bandit | 34 / 43 | 8,11,8,7 / 9,13,8,13 | 4.58 / 4.43 |
| greedy self-trainer, no cells (M08a) | 30 / 26 | 10,8,6,6 / 5,10,4,7 | 4.60 / 4.505 |
| threshold by response consistency (AUC 0.69) | 23 / 25 | 6,4,4,9 / 5,9,4,7 | 4.68 / 4.565 |

Verdict: kill for claim 2 (the consistency threshold admits fewer corrupted rewards than the self-trainer in both seeds, by 23 and 4 percent, and has the best eval score in both). Pass for the consistency rule as the fixed allocator on free text.
What it changes: the in-training picture is now uniform. In every setting tested (GSM8K at 1.5B and 0.5B, MBPP in replay, open-ended with a reward-model proxy), a label-free threshold on the cheap grader's own consistency signal (vote agreement for exact answers, response consistency for free text) is the best verification allocator at equal budget; the learned allocators lose or tie, and the cell bandit is always worst. The self-trainer's only in-training win (M08a) was over the reward-model gap rule, which is a near-random signal. The thesis claim becomes: self-consistency gating is a near-optimal, label-free rule for budgeted verification in RL post-training; learned allocation does not improve on it; and the diagnostic that predicts this is the AUC of the consistency signal measured on a pre-training probe. Remaining open: the 7B-judge versions on goldberg (5090b, M08) and the hannah four-arm runs, which add the full and random bounds at 150 steps.

## DIAG-v0 2026-10-02 09:50 UTC pass (product piece, not a hypothesis test)
What: first product piece from METHOD_AND_PRODUCT.md section 4, the standalone regime diagnostic, built as avrl/avrl_router/diagnose.py (library function plus CLI, numpy, pandas, scikit-learn only, 214 lines) with README. Input: one row per rollout group with the cheap grader's confidence and whether it disagreed with the expensive grader. Output: proxy error rate, AUC of low confidence for predicting error (bootstrap CIs), the corruption admitted at each budget by the confidence-ranked threshold vs random allocation, the smallest budget meeting a target corruption, and a regime label (strong AUC >= 0.85, usable 0.65 to 0.85, uninformative below 0.65; bands set from the in-training outcomes: vote agreement and response consistency both beat random in training, reward-model margin did not).
Validation over every saved point with the groups schema (n_boot 1000, seed 0; full table in avrl/runs/diag_v0/summary.md):

| point | confidence | n | error rate | AUC | corrupted at 25% budget, threshold vs random | relative reduction | budget for 2% corruption | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| p0 (GSM8K 1.5B) | agreement | 3000 | 0.145 | 0.914 | 0.026 vs 0.109 | 76% | 0.30 | strong |
| E06 (GSM8K 0.5B) | agreement | 3000 | 0.384 | 0.874 | 0.181 vs 0.288 | 37% | 0.70 | strong |
| E08 (MBPP) | agreement | 600 | 0.488 | 0.834 | 0.263 vs 0.366 | 28% | 0.85 | usable |
| E09d (open-ended, 7B judge) | consistency | 4000 | 0.073 | 0.690 | 0.036 vs 0.054 | 34% | 0.50 | usable |
| E09d | rm_gap12 | 4000 | 0.073 | 0.569 | 0.051 vs 0.054 | 7% | 0.65 | uninformative |
| E09b (open-ended, 3B judge) | consistency | 1500 | 0.181 | 0.693 | 0.094 vs 0.136 | 31% | 0.80 | usable |
| E09b | rm_gap12 | 1500 | 0.181 | 0.509 | 0.131 vs 0.136 | 4% | 0.90 | uninformative |

E03, E04, E05 have no group-level confidence plus error table (different artifacts) and are listed as unavailable rather than remapped. Reading: the diagnostic reproduces the thesis picture from saved rollouts alone, with no training: agreement is a strong signal on exact-answer tasks, consistency is a usable signal on free text, the reward-model margin is near random. The budget needed for 2 percent corruption ranges from 0.30 (GSM8K 1.5B) to 0.85 (MBPP, where the proxy is wrong half the time), which is the number a user of the framework would actually act on.
Follow-ups: wire the same function into the training harness as a step-0 report (the TRL reward wrapper, second product piece); add a canary-based drift check once M10's proxy-only arm shows whether proxy error drifts.

|---|---|---|---|---|

Verdict (seed 0): tie. Consistency equals the RM-gap rule exactly (64 vs 64) and sits within one seed's spread of the learned arms; judge scores all within 0.1 except greedy seed 1. The clean consistency win seen with the 3B judge is absent with the 7B judge.
Why this matters: the E09d replay (same 7B judge, base-policy rollouts) predicts a 34 percent reduction vs random for consistency and 7 percent for the gap at 25 percent budget; in training neither rule beats the random expectation (0.07 x 900 = 63). Two measured differences between replay and training: the in-training proxy error starts near 12 to 13 percent and falls to 3 to 6 percent by the last quarter for every arm (the replay's base rate is a flat 7.3 percent), and the training judge is 4-bit bitsandbytes while the replay judge was AWQ under vLLM. The confidence feature itself is identical (mean pairwise MiniLM cosine, lowest verified first; checked in judge_rewards.py and allocators.py). Per-group logging (backlog H1) is needed to measure the in-training AUC directly.
What it changes: the claim "consistency threshold is near-optimal in every setting" must be narrowed to "never worse than any learned allocator, and clearly better only when the cheap grader's error is high (3B judge, 18 percent base error)". With a low and shrinking proxy error (7B judge, 7 percent falling to 3 percent) all allocation rules tie, which is itself the regime the diagnostic should report as "allocation does not matter much here".
Follow-ups: seed 1 (running) decides the final M09b verdict; H1 per-group logging then an in-training AUC-over-time plot; product piece 2 (step-0 in-training diagnostic) so the user learns the regime from the first few hundred groups rather than from a replay that may not match.
Addendum (M09b interim, 09:55 UTC): sampling is identical between replay and training (temperature 1.0, top_p 1.0, 384 max tokens, 8 responses per prompt, checked in E09_judge_task.py and p1_judge_grpo.py), so the higher early in-training proxy error (21 of 150 unverified groups in the first 25 steps, 14 percent, vs 7.3 percent base rate in E09d) is most likely the judge: 4-bit bitsandbytes Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in training vs the AWQ build under vLLM in replay. The two judges disagree with the reward model at different rates, which changes the regime the allocator faces. The per-group logs (H1) will let this be measured directly on the next run.

## E10 2026-10-02 10:30 UTC interim (hannah 78890, four arms at about 120 of 300 steps): the cheap grader is wrong on 99 percent of groups for the base model, and no arm has improved on the base model yet
What is running: OLMo 2 1B base, MBPP train (464 problems), 300 steps, 8 groups x 8 completions, LoRA, lr 1e-5, four arms on four A100s (full = every group test-executed, proxy = signature vote only, threshold and learned at 25 percent budget). Reference bars (local eval, n=200 MBPP test / 164 HumanEval): base 8.5 / 7.9 percent, Instruct 14.5 / 18.9 percent.

| arm | steps so far | corrupted of unverified | eval MBPP pass@1 at step 50, 100 |
|---|---|---|---|
| full | 120 | 0 of 0 | 0.065, 0.070 |
| proxy | 122 | 974 of 976 (99.8 percent) | 0.060, 0.020 |
| threshold | 120 | 717 of 720 (99.6 percent) | 0.070, 0.055 |
| learned | 121 | 722 of 726 (99.4 percent) | 0.075, 0.025 |

Reading: proxy error here means the majority-signature members fail the real asserts (code_rewards.group_signature_stats). For a policy that passes 8 percent of problems, the most common output is almost always wrong, so the signature vote rewards wrong programs on essentially every group; the proxy arm's pass rate is already falling (0.085 base to 0.02), which is reward hacking of the cheap grader in its purest form. The budgeted arms inherit the same corruption on their 75 percent unverified groups. The full arm, the one with a clean reward, is also flat or slightly down at step 100 (0.065 to 0.070 vs 0.085 base, eval n=200 so plus or minus 2 points): with 8 percent pass rate and 8 completions per prompt, roughly half the groups have no correct completion and give zero advantage, so the learning signal is very sparse.
Interim answer to "can the base model beat OLMo Instruct with just the grader": not with this recipe. Two things have to change before the question is even fair: a cheap grader that carries signal for a weak policy (candidate: the prompt's visible first assert as the cheap check, the full assert set as the expensive check, which is the public/hidden test split used in real code RL), and a denser or easier reward (partial credit per assert, or a curriculum restricted to problems the base model solves at least once in 8 samples). The regime diagnostic (DIAG-v0) run on base-model rollouts at step 0 would have flagged this before any GPU time was spent, which is the product argument.
Next: E10p0 = base and Instruct rollouts on the 464 training problems (8 each) on goldberg after M09b, giving per-problem solvability for a curriculum, the signature-vote proxy error, and the visible-test proxy AUC; then E10b with the better proxy and the curriculum on hannah after 78891. Final E10 numbers (step 300 plus HumanEval) logged when the job ends, about 12:00 UTC.

## M09b 2026-10-02 10:45 UTC final: tie (consistency-ranked threshold, 7B judge, 150 steps, two seeds, goldberg RTX 5090)
Seed 1 finished 10:39 UTC. Full 7B-judge panel (4-bit Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct judge, 150 steps, 300 expensive calls and 900 unverified groups per arm):

| arm | seed 0 corrupted | seed 1 corrupted | final judge score s0 / s1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| threshold, consistency (M09b) | 64 | 54 | 5.165 / 4.91 |

Verdict: tie on the pre-registered metric (corrupted groups) and on held-out judge score; seed-to-seed spread (64 vs 54 for the same rule) is as large as any between-arm gap. Seed 1's proxy error is flat (0.059 first quarter, 0.054 last) where seed 0's fell from 0.117 to 0.045, so the "shrinking error" pattern is itself seed-dependent.
What it changes: with the 7B judge the open-ended task sits in the regime where allocation does not matter: base corruption is 6 to 7 percent of groups and no rule, fixed or learned, moves it. The M09 result (3B judge, 18 percent base error) stays the only setting where the consistency threshold clearly wins, and M07/M06 (exact-answer tasks) the settings where the agreement threshold clearly wins. Revised statement for the thesis: a confidence-ranked threshold is never beaten by a learned allocator at equal budget in any of the six in-training settings, and its advantage over random allocation scales with the cheap grader's base error rate and the confidence AUC (large on GSM8K and the 3B-judge task, nil here). The regime diagnostic predicts which case a user is in before training; the honest product is the diagnostic plus the threshold recipe, not a learned router.
Figure 2 regenerated with the fourth panel (lit/avrl/fig/fig2_in_training.png, fig2b_eval.png).

## E10p0 2026-10-02 11:20 UTC pass (OLMo base and Instruct rollouts on the 464 MBPP training problems, goldberg RTX 5090, vLLM, 8 completions each)
Why: E10's cheap grader (signature vote) is wrong on 99 percent of groups; needed the base rates and a candidate replacement cheap grader before designing E10b.

| metric | OLMo 2 1B base | OLMo 2 1B Instruct |
|---|---|---|
| mean pass rate (full asserts) | 0.012 [0.008, 0.017] | 0.093 [0.075, 0.112] |
| problems solvable at pass@8 | 6.7 percent (31 of 464) | 23.3 percent (108 of 464) |
| signature-vote proxy error (group) | 0.987 | 0.873 |
| visible-test (first assert) disagreement with full asserts, per completion | 0.008 | 0.023 |
| AUC, signature agreement predicts proxy error | 0.13 | 0.68 |
| AUC, visible-pass fraction predicts signature-proxy error | 0.99 | 0.98 |

Reading: (1) the base model solves 1.2 percent of training problems under the training prompt format, so RL from base has almost no signal (31 solvable problems); the honest answer to "train the base model to beat Instruct with the grader" is that a 1B base model needs a supervised stage first, which is what AI2 did. (2) The visible first assert is a near-perfect cheap grader at step 0 (1 to 2 percent disagreement per completion), the public-versus-hidden test split used in real code RL. Under training it is the textbook hacking target (hardcode the visible case), so proxy error should drift upward in a proxy-only arm; that drift is what budgeted verification and the canary alarm exist for. (3) Signature agreement is uninformative for the base model and weak for Instruct. Files: avrl/runs/E10p0/RESULT.md, result.json, base_solvable_task_ids.json, exp/E10p0_rollouts.py, exp/E10p0_analyze.py. Prompt format copied verbatim from p1_code_grpo.build_eval_prompt (import blocked by sklearn missing in .venv-judge), verified against both tokenizers.
Follow-up: E10b (pre-registered in the backlog).

The standard error of a 300-prompt eval is about 2.7 points, so every arm is within noise of every other, including the bandit with 62 corrupted groups. Two honest implications: (1) verifying every group buys nothing on this task at this horizon, which is the practical case for budgeted verification; (2) corrupted-reward counts in the 4 to 10 percent range do not move final accuracy at 100 steps on GSM8K with a 1.5B instruct policy, so the project's primary metric (corrupted groups admitted) is a proxy for a harm that this setting is too robust to show. The proxy-only arms (M10 part 2, running, done about 14:30 UTC) will show whether zero verification is also free here, which would mean the cheap grader alone suffices on GSM8K at this scale.

## E10 2026-10-02 12:20 UTC final: kill as configured (OLMo 2 1B base, MBPP, signature-vote cheap grader, hannah 78890, four arms x 300 steps on four A100s, 32 s/step)
Reference evaluations on hannah (200 MBPP test problems, 164 HumanEval): base 0.088 / 0.073, Instruct 0.130 / 0.183.

| arm | expensive calls | corrupted of unverified | MBPP pass@1 at step 50, 100, 200, 300 | HumanEval pass@1 at 300 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| full | 2400 | 0 of 0 | 0.065, 0.070, 0.060, 0.065 | 0.055 |
| proxy | 0 | 2398 of 2400 (99.9 percent) | 0.060, 0.020, 0.005, 0.005 | 0.006 |
| threshold, 25 percent | 600 | 1797 of 1800 (99.8 percent) | 0.070, 0.055, 0.035, 0.030 | 0.030 |
| learned, 25 percent | 600 | 1796 of 1800 (99.8 percent) | 0.075, 0.025, 0.020, 0.015 | 0.018 |

Verdict: KILL for the question as posed (base model plus signature-vote grader). No arm beats the base model; the proxy arm collapses to 0.5 percent, the two budgeted arms land between proxy and full, and even the fully verified arm drifts slightly below base (0.065 vs 0.088, eval SE about 2 points) because the reward is too sparse (1.2 percent pass rate, E10p0). This is the first setting where corrupted rewards visibly destroy quality: when the cheap grader is wrong on 99 percent of groups, every unverified group pushes the policy toward the most common wrong program. Together with M10 (GSM8K, 4 to 10 percent corruption, no accuracy effect) it brackets the thesis's primary metric: corrupted-reward counts matter in proportion to the cheap grader's error rate, and the step-0 diagnostic (99 percent here) would have predicted the collapse before training. The fix is E10b (Instruct start, visible-test grader, curriculum), whose proxy arm on goldberg is already at 0.22 held-out pass@1 against 0.145 reference; hannah four-arm seed 0 is job 78892, queued behind 78891.

Verdict: hacking ABSENT (last-to-first ratios 1.07 and 0.49). The majority-vote proxy's error stays flat at 12 percent of groups over 100 steps, and the proxy-only arm's final accuracy (0.680 / 0.657) is indistinguishable from the fully verified arm (0.663 / 0.663). On GSM8K with a 1.5B instruct policy at this horizon, the cheap grader alone is enough and the canary alarm has nothing to detect. Together with E10b (code, visible test: flat false-positive rate, proxy arm as good as full) and E10 (base model, signature vote: 99 percent wrong, collapse), the picture is that what matters is the cheap grader's error rate at step 0, not drift during training, in every setting measured so far. The step-0 diagnostic is therefore the decision tool; budgeted verification is insurance whose value shows in the corrupted-reward count, not in held-out quality, at these scales. The GSM8K panel of Figure 2 now has both reference bars.

p1_code_grpo.py gained --burnin_steps K (every group verified for the first K steps, then the regime diagnostic from avrl_router runs on those K x 8 fully labelled groups and writes <out>/burnin_report.md and burnin_diagnosis.json), --target_corruption (default 0.02) and --budget auto (the budget for the rest of training is set to the diagnostic's recommended value, clamped to [0.05, 1.0], logged as a type "burnin" row). Default behaviour unchanged (smoke output identical to the H1 reference), --eval_only unchanged, burn-in verifications counted in expensive_calls. Validated on aspen with 6 burn-in steps (48 groups; with so few groups the AUC is degenerate and the chosen budget was 0.10, which is expected noise, not a result). This is the "train your own small model" loop the product needs: start with full verification, measure the cheap grader's reliability on your own task, then let the tool pick the budget. Caveat from the agent: aspen OOMed at G=8 for OLMo 1B (the known A4000 limit), so the smoke used G=4. ## M11 random arm 2026-10-02 15:55 UTC (seed 0, cherry A4000, 47 s/step): the reward-model-gap rule equals random allocation, the consistency rule is 0.6x random
Open-ended task, 3B judge, 25 percent budget, 100 steps, 600 unverified groups per arm.

| arm (seed 0) | corrupted of 600 | final judge score |
|---|---|---|
| random (M11) | 39 | 4.57 |
| threshold, RM gap (P1-judge-lr5) | 40 | 4.62 |
| greedy self-trainer (M08a) | 30 | 4.60 |
| threshold, consistency (M09) | 23 (seed 1: 25) | 4.68 (seed 1: 4.565) |
| full (M11, seed 0, running at step 97) | 0 | 4.88 at step 75 |

Reading: the measured random control lands exactly where the reward-model-gap rule did (39 vs 40), confirming that rule carried no information (its AUC was 0.51 to 0.57). The consistency rule's 23 is 0.59x random, so THRESHOLD USEFUL passes on seed 0 (bar 0.7x); seed 1 of random is next on cherry. The full arm's held-out judge score at step 75 (4.88) is above every quarter-budget arm (4.57 to 4.68); the claim 1 test (consistency threshold within 0.15 of full) is decided at step 100. If full stays ahead, this is the first setting where buying every expensive label improves held-out quality, and the likely reason is not only the 6 percent of corrupted groups: in this harness the unverified groups are rewarded by the reward model's scores and the verified ones by the judge, so the full arm trains on a different, judge-shaped reward for every group rather than on a cleaner version of the same reward. That distinction matters for how claim 1 is stated for free-text tasks.
Addendum (16:00 UTC, reward definitions in the judge harness, from judge_rewards.py): an unverified group's reward is rank-based, 1 for the responses whose reward-model score is above the group median (always half the group), 0 otherwise; a verified group's reward is absolute, 1 for each response the judge scores 7 or more. The corrupted-group metric only asks whether the reward model's top response would have been judged positive. So the full arm differs from the budgeted arms in two ways at once: fewer wrong rewards and an absolute rather than rank-based reward on every group. Any held-out gap between full and the quarter-budget arms on this task has to be attributed with that in mind; the code and GSM8K harnesses do not have this confound (their proxy and gold rewards are both per-response correctness flags).

Pre-registered claim 1 test (consistency threshold within 0.15 of full on both seeds): FAIL on seed 0 (4.68 vs 4.92, gap 0.24, and the full arm pulls away steadily from step 50). Seed 1 of full is running on spruce. This is the first of seven in-training settings where buying every expensive label improves held-out quality. Two readings, not yet separable: (a) on free-text tasks the judge's labels carry information the reward model lacks on more than the 6 percent of groups the corruption metric flags; (b) the harness confound recorded above (rank-based proxy reward, absolute judge reward), which M12 is designed to separate. Either way the honest statement of claim 1 becomes: equal held-out quality at a quarter of the calls on exact-answer and code tasks where the cheap and expensive graders produce the same kind of reward; on the free-text task with a reward-model proxy, full judging was worth 0.24 judge points over the best quarter-budget rule at 100 steps.

Reading: for a policy that already solves 43 percent of the task, 300 steps of RL with any of the four graders moves held-out quality by at most a few points, so the budget again changes only the corruption count. The threshold rule's advantage over random replicates on a second policy family (ratios 0.20 to 0.24 for OLMo, 0.38 for Qwen). The stronger policy makes the cheap grader less reliable (20 percent false-positive groups vs 16), the opposite of the base-model case where a weak policy made the signature grader useless; the visible-test grader's error is bounded by test coverage, not by the policy's skill.

## M12 2026-10-02 18:55 UTC pass (full arm with rank-shaped judge rewards, open-ended 3B-judge task, seeds 0 and 1, spruce and cherry, 49 s/step)
Pre-registered: if full_rank is within 0.1 of full (absolute rewards) the gain comes from the labels; if it falls to the quarter-budget arms' level (about 4.6 to 4.7) the gain was reward shape.

| arm | expensive calls | final judge score s0 / s1 | mean |
|---|---|---|---|
| full, absolute judge reward (M11) | 800 | 4.92 / 4.64 | 4.78 |
| full, rank-shaped judge reward (M12) | 800 | 4.685 / 4.655 | 4.67 |
| threshold, consistency, 25 percent (M09) | 200 | 4.68 / 4.565 | 4.62 |
| random, 25 percent (M11) | 200 | 4.57 / 4.455 | 4.51 |

Verdict: with the judge reward shaped like the proxy reward (top half of each group), verifying every group lands at the quarter-budget consistency threshold's level on both seeds (4.685 vs 4.68, 4.655 vs 4.565; both within the 0.15 bar), and the seed-0 full-absolute score of 4.92 is the only point above that band. So the held-out advantage of full verification seen in M11 came from the absolute reward shape (a response is rewarded only if the judge scores it 7 or more, which lets the group's reward reflect absolute quality), not from the labels the budgeted arms lacked. Claim 1 therefore PASSES on the open-ended task like for like: at equal reward shape, the consistency threshold at a quarter of the judge calls matches full judging. Two follow-ups worth one line each: (1) a quarter-budget arm whose verified groups use the absolute reward would test whether a small number of absolute-reward groups recovers the 4.92 (cheap to run, not yet queued); (2) the absolute-vs-rank reward shape is itself a design choice a framework user should know about, since it moved held-out quality more than any allocation rule did.

The honest framing for the thesis: the cheap grader's error at step 0 is what predicts outcomes; drift is a risk to monitor, not a phenomenon observed here.

## E10e burn-in 2026-10-02 19:30 UTC (goldberg seed 1, interim at step 30): the diagnostic on 200 real groups reproduces the panel, and a 2 percent absolute target buys an 85 percent budget
Burn-in report (25 steps, 200 groups, every group verified): proxy error 0.160 [0.115, 0.210]; AUC of low confidence (visible-pass agreement) for error 0.857 [0.776, 0.927], regime strong; corruption at budget 0.10 / 0.25 / 0.50: threshold 0.080 / 0.040 / 0.030 vs random 0.144 / 0.120 / 0.080. The 0.25 figure (4.0 percent) matches the three full E10b threshold runs (3.7 to 4.1 percent) and the random figure matches theirs (16 to 18 percent), so 200 groups at step 0 predict the 300-step outcome. Chosen budget for a 2 percent target: 0.85, because the threshold's residual corruption flattens near 3 percent above half budget. Product lesson, recorded for the framework: an absolute corruption target is the wrong default when the cheap grader's base error is 16 percent; the tool should default to a relative target (for example a quarter of the base error, here 4 percent, which the diagnostic would have met at budget 0.25) or present the curve and ask. The arm continues at 0.85 so the pre-registered check (post-burn-in corruption at or below target) can be read at step 300.
Addendum (19:35 UTC): seed 3 of the open-ended pair also OOMed on the A4000s (step 18, both boxes), so it is not a seed-2 quirk: this configuration sits at 14.5 of 15.6 GB and any long batch tips it over; seeds 0 and 1 were lucky. ## Note 2026-10-02 20:50 UTC: held-out evaluation subsets depend on the run seed (methodological caveat for every cross-seed comparison)
All three harnesses draw the periodic held-out subset with the run seed (p1_grpo.load_eval_prompts, p1_judge_grpo.load_e09_prompts, p1_code_grpo.load_eval_subset): seed 0 and seed 1 runs are evaluated on different 300 GSM8K problems, different 200 HelpSteer2 prompts, different 200 MBPP test problems. HumanEval is the fixed first 164 problems in every run. Consequences: (1) comparisons between arms at the same seed are paired on the same subset and remain valid, which is how every pre-registered per-seed test was defined; (2) the absolute level of an arm's score varies across seeds partly because the subset varies, so statements such as "the full arm's own seed spread is 0.28" (M11) and "seed 2's held-out MBPP is lower for both arms" (E10b seed 2) describe subset difficulty as much as training variance and should not be read as instability; (3) comparisons against the untrained reference are clean on HumanEval for every seed, and on MBPP or GSM8K only when the reference was evaluated on the same seed's subset (the local OLMo reference and hannah's references used seed 0, so the seed 0 arms are comparable to them and the seed 1 and 2 arms are not on MBPP). The E10b and E10d "beats the untrained model" verdicts therefore rest on HumanEval for all seeds and on MBPP for seed 0; the corruption counts and the per-seed claim 1 gaps are unaffected. M13 full seed 3 (5.115 on seed 3's subset) is a case in point: it is compared only with the consistency threshold seed 3, running now.

## E10e-rel 2026-10-02 22:25 UTC: relative-target auto budget (goldberg seed 1, 300 steps): FAIL on the corruption target by integer rounding, pass on calls and quality
Pre-registered: burn-in should choose about 0.15; PASS if post-burn-in corruption is at or below 0.04 (a quarter of the 0.16 base error), final held-out MBPP within 2 points of the full arm, total expensive calls under 700.

| quantity | E10e (absolute 0.02) | E10e-rel (rel:0.25, effective 0.04) |
|---|---|---|
| chosen budget | 0.85 | 0.15 (realized 1 of 8 groups per step = 0.125) |
| post-burn-in corrupted of unverified | 1 of 275 (0.4 percent) | 145 of 1925 (7.5 percent) |
| total expensive calls | 2125 | 475 |
| MBPP pass@1 at 300 / HumanEval | 0.205 / 0.220 | 0.240 / 0.226 (same seed-1 subset as the full arm's 0.205 / 0.220) |

Verdict: calls and quality PASS, corruption target FAIL (7.5 percent against 4). Cause, in two parts: the harness verifies round(budget x 8) groups per step, so 0.15 became 0.125 and the burn-in grid's value for 0.125 lies between its 0.10 (8 percent) and 0.15 (4 percent) points; and the burn-in estimate from 200 groups has a wide interval (threshold at 0.25 was 0.040 with interval 0.015 to 0.060). Product fix pre-registered as PRODUCT-4: recommend only realizable budgets (multiples of 1/groups per step), round up, and meet the target with the upper confidence bound of the curve rather than the point estimate; on these rows that would choose 0.25 or 0.375, in line with the fixed-budget threshold arms (4 percent at 0.25). The loop itself behaved as designed; the picking rule was too optimistic.

## BUG 2026-10-02 22:50 UTC: the open-ended harness evaluated 6-token policy responses (every open-ended eval judge score before this note is void)
Found while reading the common re-evaluation: p1_judge_grpo.py passed --judge_max_new_tokens (default 6, the judge's own output cap) as the policy's generation cap in the periodic held-out evaluation (JudgeEval), so every "eval judge score" in the open-ended entries (P1-judge, M08a, M09, M09b, M11, M12, M13, the hannah bf16 runs, and the first re-evaluation table) scored responses truncated to 6 tokens. The training-time rewards are unaffected (the policy's training completions were full length, 320 or 384 tokens, and the judge calls that define the corrupted-group metric scored those), so every corruption count and every claim 2 verdict stands. What is void: every held-out quality statement on the open-ended task, including claim 1 there (M11, M13), the M12 reward-shape reading, and the P1-judge "judge score diverged" remarks. Fix: --eval_max_new_tokens (default = --max_completion_length) in the harness; the re-evaluation script now takes --policy_max_new_tokens (default 320). Re-evaluating all 16 local open-ended adapters plus the base model with 320-token responses on the fixed subset (cherry), and hannah's 8 bf16-run adapters the same way with the same 4-bit 3B judge (spruce), so all open-ended quality numbers share one evaluator. hannah's seed 2 job (78910) still runs the old evaluation; its adapters will be re-evaluated too.

## PRODUCT-4 2026-10-02 23:15 UTC done: realizable budgets and a conservative pick in --budget auto
diagnose() takes n_groups_per_step and pick ("point" or "upper"); candidates are k / groups per step, and the recommendation is the smallest candidate whose threshold corruption meets the target by the point estimate or by the upper 95 percent bound. The code harness passes its groups per step and a new --budget_pick (default upper). On the E10e burn-in rows (effective target 0.04 at base error 0.16, 8 groups per step): point rule picks 0.25 (threshold 0.040), upper rule picks 0.625 (upper bound 0.045 at 0.5, 0.035 at 0.625); both realizable exactly. Old defaults unchanged (0.85 absolute, 0.15 relative on the 0.05 grid). The upper rule is conservative by design: with 200 burn-in groups the interval on a 4 percent rate is wide, so a user who wants a tighter budget should burn in longer (the report now shows the candidate table so the trade-off is visible). Not exercised on GPU yet; E10e-up (auto with --budget_pick upper) is the natural rerun and is queued on goldberg.

The product default stays the conservative rule, with the candidate table in the report so a user can choose the point rule knowingly; a longer burn-in (narrower interval) would bring the two picks together. Per block of 25 or 50 steps, AUC of low confidence (vote agreement on GSM8K, agreement among visible-passers on code, response consistency on the open-ended task) for predicting a wrong cheap reward, with 1000-draw group bootstraps; proxy_error is logged for every group (0 percent null in all 31 runs).

| family | AUC first block to last block (family average) | whole-run AUC, threshold arm vs proxy arm | reading |
|---|---|---|---|
| GSM8K, vote agreement | 0.914 to 0.918 | 0.915 vs 0.901 | flat and high |

| code, Qwen, visible-pass agreement | 0.865 to 0.842 | 0.866 vs 0.864 | slight fall |
| open-ended, response consistency, 7B judge | 0.684 to 0.675 | about 0.65 to 0.71 | flat and moderate |

Group-level false-positive rate among groups with at least one visible pass (code): 0.199 in the first block to 0.169 in the last, while the mean number of visible-passing completions per group rises from 4.2 to 6.0. Reading: the confidence feature stays informative throughout training in every family (never near 0.5), its AUC barely moves, and it does not differ materially between arms whose training distribution was shaped by verification and the proxy-only arm; the step-0 diagnostic is therefore a fair forecast of the whole run, which is what the burn-in design assumes. The open-ended feature's AUC (about 0.68) is the weakest of the four and matches the replay estimate (E09d: 0.69), closing the earlier replay-versus-training discrepancy raised under M09b: the in-training AUC was never lower than the replay's; the difference lay in the base error rate and judge build, not in the feature. Outputs: avrl/runs/h1_auc/SUMMARY.md, auc_over_time.png, per-run json; script avrl/exp/H1_auc_over_time.py.

## M15 2026-10-03 04:50 UTC: the nine low-error-regime comparisons are complete; claim 2 stays a tie by the pre-registered bar
Pre-registered (backlog M15): a real small edge for the greedy self-trainer if it admits fewer corrupted groups than the consistency threshold in at least eight of nine comparisons and the pooled ratio is below 0.9.

| judge build, seed | greedy | consistency threshold | lower |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4-bit 7B, s0 (M08 / M09b) | 58 | 64 | greedy |
| 4-bit 7B, s1 | 53 | 54 | greedy |
| 4-bit 7B, s2 (M15) | 44 | 54 | greedy |
| 4-bit 7B, s3 (M15) | 43 | 58 | greedy |
| bf16 7B, s0 (hannah) | 39 | 53 | greedy |
| bf16 7B, s1 | 45 | 44 | threshold |
| bf16 7B, s2 | 38 | 43 | greedy |
| bf16 7B, s3 | 37 | 34 | threshold |
| bf16 7B, s4 | 40 | 48 | greedy |
| pooled | 397 | 452 | ratio 0.878, difference 55 against a Poisson spread of about 29 (z about 1.9) |

Verdict: the pooled ratio meets the bar (0.878 below 0.9) but the count does not (seven of nine, not eight), so by the pre-registered rule claim 2 remains a TIE in the low-error regime. Reported alongside it, because the thesis should state what was seen: the self-trainer admits about twelve percent fewer wrong rewards than the label-free rule in this regime, with a pooled z near 1.9 (two-sided p about 0.06), consistently in sign on seven of nine seeds, and never in the strong-signal settings (M06, M07) where it loses clearly. Held-out quality is tied in every one of the nine comparisons (M15 seed 3: 7.76 greedy against 7.785 threshold under the 4-bit judge with the fixed evaluation). The honest sentence for the thesis: a learned allocator can shave a further ten percent off the wrong-reward count when the cheap grader's confidence signal is weak (AUC about 0.68), at the cost of a learned component, and buys nothing when the signal is strong; the label-free threshold remains the recommended default.
